[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
.
So, let
r
be a reward of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
let
(s,h)
be a pair not in
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}\supseteq\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, for
h\in\llbracket H-1\rrbracket
.
Notice that the existence of pair
(s,h)
is guaranteed by hypothesis.
For what we have seen at the previous step, for any pair
of actions
a_{1},a_{2}
, for any
p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
, it holds that

Q_{h}^{*}(s,a_{1};p^{\prime},r)=Q_{h}^{*}(s,a_{2};p^{\prime},r)
.
Through the Bellman’s equation, we can write:

r_{h}(s,a_{1})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a_{1})}[V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)]=r_{h}(s,a_{2})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a_{2})}[V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)].

(10)

Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
. By definition of
\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}
,
this condition must hold for any
p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a_{1})\in\Delta^{\mathcal{S}}

and
p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a_{2})\in\Delta^{\mathcal{S}}
.
In particular, let us take two transition models
p^{1},p^{2}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}

such that
:

\displaystyle p^{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}p^{1}_{h}(s_{1}|s,a_{1})=1\\
p^{1}_{h}(s_{1}|s,a_{2})=1\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle p^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}p^{2}_{h}(s_{1}|s,a_{1})=1\\
p^{2}_{h}(s_{1}|s,a_{2})=0\\
\end{cases}.

Inserting
p^{1}
into Eq.
10
, we get:

r_{h}(s,a_{1})+V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{1};p^{1},r)=r_{h}(s,a_{2})+V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{1},p^{1},r)\implies r_{h}(s,a_{1})=r_{h}(s,a_{2}).

Because
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are arbitrary, this holds for any action
a\in\mathcal{A}
in
(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.
Therefore, we have shown that (the condition at
H
is trivial since
V_{H+1}^{*}(s;p^{\prime},r)=0
):

r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\implies\exists\{k_{h}\}_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}:\;\forall(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}},\,\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;r_{h}(s,a)=k_{h}.

Inserting
p^{2}
into Eq.
10
, we obtain:

r_{h}(s,a_{1})+V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{1};p^{2},r)=r_{h}(s,a_{2})+V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{2};p^{2},r)\implies V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{1};p^{2},r)=V_{h+1}^{*}(s_{2};p^{2},r),

since
r_{h}(s,a_{1})=r_{h}(s,a_{2})
. Because
p^{2}
(and so the next state
s_{1}
) can be chosen arbitrarily
in
h+1\geq 2
, then we have proved that:

r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\implies\exists\{\bar{V}_{h}\}_{h\in\llbracket 2,H\rrbracket}:\;\forall p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}},\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket:\;V^{*}_{h}(s;p^{\prime},r)=\bar{V}_{h}.

In a similar manner, we can prove the same result also for
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
.

The next step of the proof consists in showing that, for any
h\in\llbracket 2,H\rrbracket
,
for any
s\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h}
, the reward value assigned to expert’s action coincides with

k_{h}
.
Let
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
with
h\geq 2
. For any
p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
, it holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{V}_{h}=V^{*}_{h}(s;p^{\prime},r)=Q_{h}^{*}(s,a^{E};p^{\prime},r)&=r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}[V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)]\\
&=r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}[\bar{V}_{h+1}]\\
&=r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\bar{V}_{h+1}.\\
\end{split}

(11)

By hypothesis, there exists
s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}
such that, for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{V}_{h}=V^{*}_{h}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)=Q_{h}^{*}(s^{\prime},a;p^{\prime},r)&=r_{h}(s^{\prime},a)+\E\limits_{s^{\prime\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s^{\prime},a)}[V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime\prime};p^{\prime},r)]\\
&=k_{h}+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s^{\prime},a)}[\bar{V}_{h+1}]\\
&=k_{h}+\bar{V}_{h+1}.\\
\end{split}

(12)

Comparing Eq.
11
and Eq.
12
, we
infer that
r_{h}(s,a)=k_{h}
.

With a similar reasoning, we can prove that the reward value of non-expert’s action
shall be at most
k_{h}
.
For simplicity, set
\bar{V}_{H+1}=0
. Let
(s,h)
be any pair in
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}

and let
a
be any non-expert’s action. Then, for any
p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
, we have:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}[V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)]

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\bar{V}_{h+1}

\displaystyle\leq V^{*}_{h}(s;p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s))+\bar{V}_{h+1}

\displaystyle=k_{h}+\bar{V}_{h+1}.

From which it follows that
r_{h}(s,a)\leq k_{h}
.
This concludes the proof of the sufficiency.

With regards to the necessity, we have to show that any reward
r
that can be expressed
as in the statement of the theorem belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
.
It is easy to notice that, irrespective of the transition model
p^{\prime}
,
the optimal value function of any state
s\in\mathcal{S}
at stage
h\geq 2
is

V^{*}_{h}(s;p^{\prime},r)=\sum_{i=h}^{H}k_{i}
, and that it is achieved by playing
the expert’s policy. At step
h=1
, since all next states take on the same optimal
value function, the result is immediate.
∎

Appendix C
On the Learnability of the Feasible Set

In this section, we formalize the notion of (PAC-)
learnability
and we analyze the learnability
properties of the various objects that we introduced in Section
3
(and in Appendix
B
).
Specifically, we show that both the definitions of
feasible set

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
are not
learnable
in the setting of Section
3

unless the behavioral policy covers the entire
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
space. Next, we demonstrate that
the framework we have introduced cannot be improved; simply put, we show that

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
are the
tightest learnable

bounds of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
according to partial order
\subseteq
, and that

\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
are the
tightest learnable

bounds of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.

We give a definition of
learnability
in the context of the Probably Approximately
Correct (PAC) framework since our main focus is on PAC bounds to the sample complexity in this work.
Let
\phi\in\Phi
be our target of learning, i.e., the quantity that we aim to estimate,
and let
\mu
be a certain distribution that provides us with
N
independent samples

X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc,X_{N}\sim\mu
. Intuitively,

\phi
can be
learned
from
\mu
if there exists a procedure able
to use the samples
X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc,X_{N}
of
\mu
to create a “good-enough”
estimate of
\phi
, where the “goodness” is measured by a meaningful notion
of distance. We formalize the intuition in the following definition.

Definition C.1

(PAC-learnability)
.

A quantity
\phi\in\Phi
is PAC-learnable from a distribution
\mu

if there exists a semimetric
d
in
\Phi
, that satisfies a
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality with finite
\rho
, and an algorithm
\mathfrak{A}

such that, for any
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
,
there exists a finite
N\in\mathbb{N}
for which:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\bigl(d(\phi,\widehat{\phi}\leq\epsilon)\bigr)\geq 1-\delta,

where
\widehat{\phi}\in\Phi
is the estimate of
\phi

computed by
\mathfrak{A}
using at least
N
samples, and
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
is the
probability measure induced by
\mu
.

Simply put,
\phi
is
PAC-learnable
if the samples from
\mu
leak “enough” information
about
\phi
.
Notice that any metric satisfies a
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality with
\rho=1
. See Appendix
I
for
an in-depth analysis of the semimetrics used in this work.

In the context of Section
3
, we identify as quantity of
interest
\phi
the
feasible set

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
(
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
), and as distribution
generating samples
14
14

14

Observe that, to avoid mentioning the creation of a mixture of distributions
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{E}}

(because of the two datasets), we assume that
\pi^{E}
and
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
are given and need not to be learned.
While this simplifies the learning problem, notice that even the estimation of the transition model alone
can end up in non-learnability issues.

\mu=\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
.
We have the following result of
non-learnability
of the
feasible set

in the
offline
setting.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,\mu_{0},H\rangle
be an MDP without reward and let
\pi^{E}
be the deterministic expert policy.
Assume to know
\pi^{E}
in all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
and also to know
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, i.e., there is no need to learn them.
Let
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
denote the portion of space covered by a behavioral distribution
\pi^{b}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
If
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\neq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
, then
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
is not PAC-learnable from
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
.
Moreover, if
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\neq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
and for at least one
(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}
there
exists a policy
\pi\in\Pi
such that
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi}(s_{h}=s)>0
,
then not even
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
is PAC-learnable from
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
.

Proof.

Let us start with
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.
The idea is to construct two problem instances whose feasible sets
lie at a fixed non-zero distance and such that
samples do not allow to discriminate between them.

We start with the construction of the two instances.
Let
\pi^{E}
be the expert’s policy and let
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p^{1},\mu_{0},H\rangle
be an MDP without reward in which policy
\pi^{b}

induces the distribution over trajectories
\mathbb{P}_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}
.
By hypothesis, there exists triple
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
not in
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
, i.e., such that

\mathbb{P}_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}(s_{h}=s,a_{h}=a)=0
.
Let us construct another problem instance
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p^{2},\mu_{0},H\rangle

such that
p^{2}\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}p^{1}
. This is possible by simply setting, at triple
(s,a,h)
,
the condition
p^{1}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\neq p^{2}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
, and equality elsewhere. Observe that, because of this choice,

\mathbb{P}_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}=\mathbb{P}_{p^{2},\pi^{b}}
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
denote, respectively,
the feasible sets of instances
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
with expert’s policy
\pi^{E}
.

Now we show that
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
. To do so, we claim the existence a reward
r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}

such that
r\notin\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
. W.l.o.g., assume
a
be a non-expert’s action
15
15

15

Otherwise, we can make the same construction with any non-expert’s action
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
,
and we can show that the constraints of the feasible sets of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}

have the same lower bounds
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{1}},r)=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{2}},r)
,
but different upper bounds
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{1}},r)\neq Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{2}},r)
.

. By definition of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}
,
at triple
(s,a,h)
, it holds that
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a;p^{1},r)\leq Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p^{1},r)
. Since
p^{2}
coincides with

p^{1}
everywhere except for triple
(s,a,h)
, the constraint is equivalent to
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a;p^{1},r)\leq Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{2}},r)
.
Similarly, we have that
r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
if
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a;p^{2},r)\leq Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p^{2},r)
.
Clearly, both the constraints have the same upper bound
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p^{2},r)
, and since

Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},\pi^{E}_{h+1}(s^{\prime});{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{1}},r)=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},\pi^{E}_{h+1}(s^{\prime});{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{2}},r)
for any
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
, then
p^{1}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\neq p^{2}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)

entails
Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{1}},r)\neq Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{2}},r)
. Therefore, we can find
r\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}
such that
r\notin\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
,
thus
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
.

We proceed by contradiction. Let us assume that the feasible set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
is PAC-learnable
in both
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. By definition of learnability, there exists a semi-metric
d
and an algorithm
\mathfrak{A}

with certain properties. By definition of semi-metric, since
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
, then
there exists a certain
c>0
such that
d(\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})=c
. Moreover, by

\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality,
we know that a set of rewards
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
such that

d(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}})<c/(2\rho)
and
d(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})<c/(2\rho)
at the same time does not exist,
thus the two events
\bigl\{d(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}})<c/(2\rho)\bigr\}
and
\bigl\{d(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})<c/(2\rho)\bigr\}

are disjoint.
By the choices
\epsilon<c/(2\rho)
and
\delta<1/2
, algorithm
\mathfrak{A}
must satisfy

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}\Bigl(d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\Bigr)>\frac{1}{2}\quad\quad\wedge\quad\quad\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{2},\pi^{b}}\Bigl(d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\Bigr)>\frac{1}{2}.

By construction, we have
\mathbb{P}_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}=\mathbb{P}_{p^{2},\pi^{b}}
. In other words, samples do not allow
to discriminate between instances
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, and so between
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}

and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
. Therefore, when faced with
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, independently on the number
N
of samples,
algorithm
\mathfrak{A}
outputs

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}}
such that:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}\biggl(

\displaystyle\bigl\{d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\bigr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}\biggl(d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\biggr)}_{>1/2}+\underbrace{\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{1},\pi^{b}}\biggl(d(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathfrak{A}},\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}})<\frac{c}{2\rho}\biggr)}_{>1/2}>1,

where we have used that the two events are disjoint.
This is clearly a contradiction, thus the statement of the theorem holds for the notion of feasible set
in Definition
3.1
.

With regards to the novel notion of feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, the proof is analogous. The only difference is in how to show that

\mathcal{R}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
when the triple
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
is such that

(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}
. Indeed, by Definition
3.2
, in such
(s,h)
there is no constraint
on which action shall be optimal. However, by the hypothesis contained in the statement of the theorem,
there exists a policy that brings to
(s,h)
, so since
\pi^{b}
does not reach
(s,h)
, then there exists
another triple
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, with
h^{\prime}<h
, such that
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime},h^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
and
(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.
Therefore, the same passages adopted to show that
\mathcal{R}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}

can be used to show also that
\mathcal{R}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
.
It should be remarked that the hypothesis
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\neq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
alone
is not sufficient
16
16

16

Consider for instance the MDP without reward
\mathcal{M}
in which
\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket\setminus\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}=\{(\bar{s},1)\}
and

\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}=\{(\bar{s},a_{1},1),\dotsc,(\bar{s},a_{A},1)\}
, i.e.,
that
\pi^{b}
covers the entire space except for a state
\bar{s}
at stage
h=1
(
\mu_{0}(\bar{s})=0
).
Clearly, such state does not appear in the constraints defining the feasible set, and the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
is learnable by
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
!

for the non-learnability of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

The following theorem demonstrates that the solution concepts (subset and superset) that we propose in our
framework are the
tightest learnable
. Observe that Theorem
C.2
entails Theorem
C.1
. However,
since in the proof of Theorem
C.2
we make use of the construction
introduced in the proof of Theorem
C.1
, we prefer to keep the two theorems separated.

Theorem C.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,\mu_{0},H\rangle
be an MDP without reward and let
\pi^{E}
be the deterministic expert policy.
Assume to know
\pi^{E}
in all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
and also to know
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, i.e., there is no need to learn them.
Let
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
denote the portion of space covered by a behavioral distribution
\pi^{b}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
Then,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
are, respectively, the
tightest

subset and superset of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
that can be
learned
from
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
. Moreover,

\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
are, respectively, the
tightest

subset and superset of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
that can be
learned
from
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
.

Proof.

The theorem states that the considered quantities are the tightest learnable. Thus, we split the proof in two parts.
First, we prove that such quantities are PAC-learnable, then we show that
there is no other object that is at the same time learnable and tighter.

Let us begin with
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
. By Definition
C.1
,
these quantities are PAC-learnable if we can find a semi-metric
d
between sets of rewards and an algorithm
\mathfrak{A}

such that, for any arbitrarily small choice of the accuracy
\epsilon
and confidence
\delta
, we can always find a

finite
number of samples that algorithm
\mathfrak{A}
can use to compute a set of rewards

\epsilon
-close, according to semi-metric
d
, to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(or to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
) w.h.p..
This is exactly what, for instance, Theorem
5.1
states: Algorithm
1

requires a finite number of samples to compute an
\epsilon
-correct estimate of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}

(or of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
) w.h.p. according to any of the semi-metrics presented in Definition
4.1
(for which we prove in Appendix
I
that a
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality holds).
We are not going to show that an analogous of Theorem
5.1
holds also for

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

Now we show that these quantities are the tightest learnable.
Let us start with
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
, and then we will move to
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
,
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

The idea is to construct by contradiction another concept
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(non calligraphic) of subset of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}

which is tighter than
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
, and then show that we can construct a problem instance in which
the newly defined concept
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
fails at being a subset of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
. Thus, by contradiction, let us assume that there exists
a problem instance
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,\mu_{0},H\rangle
with expert’s policy
\pi^{E}

and distribution generating samples
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
, in which there exists
a PAC-learnable set
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
from
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}

such that
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\subset\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.
If
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}=\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
, then
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}=\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, so we consider the case in which
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\subset\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
.
Let
\bar{r}
be a reward of
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
which is not present in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
.
By definition of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
, we have that

\bar{r}\notin\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
if and only if there exists
p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}

and
\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
such that
\bar{r}\notin\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
.
Therefore, similarly to the proof of Theorem
C.1
, we can construct a new
problem instance
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p^{\prime},\mu_{0},H\rangle\cup\{\pi^{\prime}\}
such that, since

\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\subset\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
.
By definition of
p^{\prime}
, we know that
\mathbb{P}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{b}}=\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{b}}
, thus
any algorithm
\mathfrak{A}
estimating the new concept of subset
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}

fails to distinguish instances
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
This means that we can choose
\epsilon,\delta
so that if
\mathfrak{A}

returns in
\mathcal{M}
a set containing
\bar{r}
, then with high probability it will return it
also in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. However,
\bar{r}\notin\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
, so we get a contradiction.

The proof for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
is analogous.
By contradiction, we claim the existence of a set
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
such that

\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}\subseteq\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
. The contradiction will be
shown by considering a reward
\bar{r}
of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
which is not in
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
,
and then constructing the problem
instance in which the feasible set contains exactly that reward, but set
\overline{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
does not (w.h.p.).

As far as
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
are concerned, the proofs are analogous
to those presented above. However, there is a detail that has to be explained.
Specifically, we have seen in the proof of Theorem
C.1
that the condition

\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\subset\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
is not a sufficient condition for
\mathcal{R}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
.
Therefore, in principle, the proof for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
)
cannot be adapted directly to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
).
However, we observe that
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\subset\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}

(respectively,
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}\subset\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
) holds strictly
if
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\neq\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
and for at least one
(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}
there
exists a policy
\pi\in\Pi
such that
\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi}(s_{h}=s)>0
.
Otherwise, it holds that
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}=\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
, as explained in Section
D.4
.
This is exactly the condition required in Theorem
C.1

for proving
\mathcal{R}_{p^{1},\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p^{2},\pi^{E}}
. Therefore, by using this
observation, we can prove the statement of the theorem also for
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.
∎

Appendix D
Further considerations

In this appendix, we collect a variety of considerations and remarks about the learning framework introduced, about the need of two datasets, alternative representations of the feasible set, and some others.

D.1
About the need of two datasets

We presented
IRLO
(and, subsequently,
PIRLO
) in the case two datasets
\mathcal{D}^{b}
and
\mathcal{D}^{E}
collected with
\pi^{b}
and
\pi^{E}
, respectively, are available. This scenario is common in previous IRL works
(
Boularias et al. 2011
)
but, although convenient for our analysis, it is not strictly necessary to achieve a meaningful sample complexity. Indeed, we remark that the expert’s dataset is employed for estimating the expert’s support
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
and policy
\pi^{E}
. This task can be anyway achieved under Assumption
2.1
using just one dataset
\mathcal{D}=\{\langle s^{b,i}_{1},a_{1}^{b,i},a_{1}^{E,i},\dots,s^{b,i}_{H-1},a_{H-1}^{b,i},a_{H-1}^{E,i},s^{b,i}_{H}\rangle\}_{i\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
playing the behavioral policy
\pi^{b}
and keeping track of the expert’s actions too. In such a case, we must require that every transition
(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s),s^{\prime})
is exercised at least once in
\mathcal{D}
. This leads to a sample complexity bound which is larger in the last constant term in which
\rho^{\pi^{E},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}_{\min}
is replaced with:

\min\Bigg\{\rho^{\pi^{E},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}_{\min},\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\\
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}:p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s))>0\end{subarray}}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s))\Bigg\}.

D.2
About the dependence on
\rho_{\min}

The majority of the results presented for the
d_{\infty}
semimetric in this paper are characterized by a dependence on the minimum non-zero visitation probability
\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}
of the behavioral policy
\pi^{b}
. This is expected since we are targeting as solution concept the
tightest learnable
subset and supersets of the feasible set. Clearly, one can further relax this requirement, accepting to target
non-tightest learnable
sets with a benefit in the sample complexity. Consider a minimum-visitation threshold
\overline{\rho}
, we define
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\overline{\rho}}=\{(s,a,h):\,\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)>\overline{\rho}\}
as the set of triples
(s,a,h)
that are visited by at least
\overline{\rho}
probability (notice that
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}=\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{0}
).
We can use this set to employ suitable equivalence
\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\overline{\rho}}}
relations over transition models to group together those that differ in triples
(s,a,h)
visited with probability smaller than
\overline{\rho}
. This allows to redefine the sub- and super-feasible sets as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\cap}_{p,\pi^{E},\overline{\rho}}\coloneqq\bigcap\limits_{p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\overline{\rho}}}}}\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{E}},\quad\mathcal{R}^{\cup}_{p,\pi^{E},\overline{\rho}}\coloneqq\bigcup\limits_{p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\overline{\rho}}}}}\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{E}}.

Obviously, by the definition of the equivalence relation, we have that
\mathcal{R}^{\cap}_{p,\pi^{E},\overline{\rho}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\cap}_{p,\pi^{E}}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\cup}_{p,\pi^{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\cup}_{p,\pi^{E},\overline{\rho}}
. Under the assumption that
\overline{\rho}\leq\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s))
for every
(s,h)
, these sets are clearly
learnable
, but lose the property of being the
tightest
ones. The advantage of targeting these feasible sets is that we can reproduce the same proofs done for the original
\mathcal{R}^{\cap}_{p,\pi^{E}}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\cup}_{p,\pi^{E}}
obtaining a smaller sample complexity that scales with
\overline{\rho}
instead of
\rho_{\min}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.

D.3
Equivalent definitions of the feasible sets

In both Definition
3.1
and Theorem
3.1
,
we represent the set of constraints defining the (old) feasible set using the Q-function of policy

\pi^{E}
or of some policy
\overline{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
. However, it is possible to provide an alternative equivalent representation based on the optimal Q-function
Q^{*}
. It is easy to notice that the old feasible set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\}.

(13)

Moreover, thanks to Lemma
E.1
, the new feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\}.

We prefer to work with the representations presented in the main paper because
the relaxations (see Section
6
) of those representations are “better” (See Appendix
G
)
than the relaxations of the representations just introduced.

As a direct consequence of Theorem
3.1
, we have the following corollary (see Appendix
E
for the proof).

Corollary D.1
.

In the setting of Definition
3.2
the feasible reward set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\bigcup\limits_{\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{\prime}}.

This corollary provides the explicit relationship between the old
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
and new
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
definitions
of feasible set. Clearly
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.
By using Corollary
D.1
,
we can rewrite
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
as
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}=\bigcap_{p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}}\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
.
Observe that in general
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\neq\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\bigcap_{p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}}
,
because the union of the intersection is different
from the intersection of the union.
Furthermore, because of the different definitions of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}

and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, we have that, in general,

\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\nsubseteq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}

i.e., the subset for the new notion of
feasible set

is not a subset of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.
Differently, with regard to the superset, it holds that

\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}\supseteq\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
.

It should be remarked that Corollary
D.1
and Theorem
3.1
are not in contradiction. Indeed, looking at the union over policies in Corollary
D.1
, one might expect an existential quantifier inside Theorem
3.1
, but we find a universal quantifier.
By using Corollary
D.1
and Eq.
13
, we can “transform” the union into an existential quantifier to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\exists\overline{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}:\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket,\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,\overline{\pi}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\},

i.e., we are representing the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
as the set of all the rewards that induce an optimal policy in
[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
. From Theorem
3.1
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall\overline{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}},\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\,\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{\overline{\pi}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{\overline{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\},

i.e., we are representing the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
as the set of all the rewards for which playing the expert’s action in
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
is the optimal strategy irrespective of the optimal action outside
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
. To put it simple, Corollary
D.1
uses the existential quantifier because it says that a certain policy in
[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
is optimal,
while Theorem
3.1
uses the universal quantifier because it does not care about which policy in
[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
is optimal, but only that the expert’s action is played in
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
. The
\exists
gives the optimal policy, while the
\forall
says that one of the policies in
[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
is optimal, without telling which.
Clearly, there is no contradiction.

D.4
A remark about non reachable states

The strict condition
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\subset\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
alone is not a sufficient condition
to have
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}\neq\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
. Indeed,
if the portion of
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
not contained into
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
is made only of

(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
for which there is no
\pi\in\Pi
such that

(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi}
for the given
p\in\mathcal{P}
, then
neither the policy nor the transition model in
(s,h)
appears in the constraints of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap},\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}},
or
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}

(when viewed using Theorem
3.1
).
In practice, the values of the rewards
r
of

\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
) in such
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}

can be chosen arbitrarily, irrespective of the reward in any other
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime},h^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
,
and therefore we have
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}=\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

D.5
An annoying corner case

To cope with the bitter lesson of Section
8
, we work with two datasets. As aforementioned, we use
\mathcal{D}^{E}
to estimate
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
and
\pi^{E}
, and we use
\mathcal{D}^{b}
to estimate
p
. However, it might happen the following situation. Let
(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}
(where
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}
is the estimate of
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
computed from
\mathcal{D}^{E}
), and let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}=\{\overline{s}\}
, i.e., dataset
\mathcal{D}^{E}
tells us that the support of the expert’s policy at
h+1
is made of state
\overline{s}
only. Let
a^{E}\coloneqq\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s)
. By using dataset
\mathcal{D}^{b}
, we might come up with the estimate of the transition model at
(s,a^{E},h)
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widehat{p}_{h}(\overline{s}|s,a^{E})>0\\
\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a^{E})>0\end{cases},

where
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
is some other state not in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}
. Clearly, this means that
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}
; however, due to finite data, dataset
\mathcal{D}^{E}
does not provide us with this information. This fact provides a contradiction between
\widehat{p}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}
. To avoid issues in the implementation of
PIRLO
, we define the confidence set
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)
(see Eq.
7
) by allowing the support of the transition model of expert’s actions to be compatible with the estimate provided by
\mathcal{D}^{b}
, i.e., we set:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\,|\,\forall(s,a,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}:\,\|p^{\prime}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{1}\leq b_{h}(s,a)\,\wedge

\displaystyle\qquad\forall(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall s^{\prime}\not\in\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\cup\,\text{supp }\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s)}\bigr):\,p^{\prime}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s))=0\Bigr\}.

Observe that the union over the support of
\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})
solves the potential issue created by the corner case described in this section. It should be remarked that, under good event
\mathcal{E}
(see Appendix
F
), it holds that
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}=\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, and therefore
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}\,\cup\,\text{supp }\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s))=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}=\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}
.

D.6
Distances
d
and
d_{\infty}
control the distance between value functions

We provide the proof of the proposition reported in Section
4
.
See
4.2

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Theorem 4.1 of
(
Metelli et al. 2023
)
. For any
s,h
and policy
\widehat{\pi}^{*}
optimal in some
\widehat{r}
, we can write:

\displaystyle V_{h}^{*}(s;r)-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;r)

\displaystyle=V_{h}^{*}(s;r)-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;r)\pm V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=\Big(V_{h}^{*}(s;r)-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;\widehat{r})\Big)+\Big(V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;\widehat{r})-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;r)\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\Big(V_{h}^{*}(s;r)-V^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{*}}}_{h}(s;\widehat{r})\Big)+\Big(V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;\widehat{r})-V^{\widehat{\pi}^{*}}_{h}(s;r)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=h}^{H}\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{p,\pi^{*}}(s_{l}=s^{\prime},a_{l}=a^{\prime}|s_{h}=s)(r_{l}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})-\widehat{r}_{l}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum\limits_{l=h}^{H}\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{p,\widehat{\pi}^{*}}(s_{l}=s^{\prime},a_{l}=a^{\prime}|s_{h}=s)(r_{l}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})-\widehat{r}_{l}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum\limits_{l=h}^{H}\|r_{l}-\widehat{r}_{l}\|_{\infty},

where at (1) we have used that
\widehat{\pi}^{*}
is optimal under
\widehat{r}
.

Multiplying both sides by
1/M(r,\widehat{r})
concludes the proof, and noticing that
\rho^{p,\pi^{b}}
permits to bound
d_{\infty}
by
d
, we get the result.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs of Section
3
and Section
4

In this section, we provide the missing proofs of Section
3
and Section
4
.

To prove Theorem
3.1
, it is useful to introduce the following lemma.

Lemma E.1
.

In the setting of Definition
3.2
, the feasible reward set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\{

\displaystyle r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\}.

Proof.

The statement of the theorem is equivalent to the necessary and sufficient condition:

\displaystyle J(\pi^{E};\mu_{0},p,r)=\max\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}J(\pi;\mu_{0},p,r)\iff\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}:\,Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)=\max\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r).

We split the proof in two parts. First we show the sufficiency, then the necessity.

Let us start with the sufficiency.
Let
r
be any reward in
\mathfrak{R}

and
p
any transition model in
\mathcal{P}
.
By contradiction, suppose that there exists a policy
\pi^{\prime}\in\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}J(\pi;\mu_{0},p,r)

for which there exists a
(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
in the union of the supports of the
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
distributions
\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot)

in which
Q^{*}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},\pi_{h^{\prime}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime});p,r)<\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};p,r)
(the notation refers to a deterministic
\pi^{\prime}

but it can be taken stochastic by computing the expected value).
Let
\pi^{*}\in\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}V^{\pi}_{h}(s;p,r)\;\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
be an auxiliary optimal policy whose existence is a widely-known result in RL
(
Puterman 1994
, see)
.
By hypothesis, it holds that:

\displaystyle J(\pi^{\prime};\mu_{0},p,r)=\max\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}J(\pi;\mu_{0},p,r)=J(\pi^{*};\mu_{0},p,r).

From the performance difference lemma
(
Kakade & Langford 2002
)
, by denoting the advantage function by
A_{h}^{\pi}(s,a;p,r)\coloneqq Q_{h}^{\pi}(s,a;p,r)-V_{h}^{\pi}(s;p,r)
, we can write:

\displaystyle J(\pi^{\prime};\mu_{0},p,r)-J(\pi^{*};\mu_{0},p,r)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl[A_{h}^{\pi^{*}}(s,a;p,r)\bigr]

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}\rho_{h^{\prime}}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})A_{h^{\prime}}^{\pi^{*}}(s^{\prime},\pi_{h^{\prime}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime});p,r)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{<}0,

where at (1) we have used that in all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket\setminus\{(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\}

the policy
\pi^{\prime}
prescribes the action greedy w.r.t.
Q^{*}
, and thus the advantage is 0,
and (2) holds by (contradiction) hypothesis.
However, by hypothesis, we know that
J(\pi^{*};\mu_{0},p,r)-J(\pi^{\prime};\mu_{0},p,r)=0
,
thus we have obtained a contradiction, so the sufficiency holds.

As far as the necessity is concerned,
let us consider again an auxiliary optimal policy
\pi^{*}

and a policy
\pi^{\prime}
such that
Q^{*}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},\pi_{h^{\prime}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime});p,r)=\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};p,r)

in the support of the
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
distributions
\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot)
. By applying the performance difference lemma, we can write:

\displaystyle J(\pi^{\prime};\mu_{0},p,r)-J(\pi^{*};\mu_{0},p,r)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{s\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot)}\bigl[A_{h}^{\pi^{*}}(s,a;p,r)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\sum\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}:\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(s,a)>0}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{\prime}}(s,a)\underbrace{A_{h}^{\pi^{*}}(s,a;p,r)}_{=0}

\displaystyle=0,

where we have simply used the hypothesis.

By setting
\pi^{E}\equiv\pi^{\prime}
, we get the result.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove Theorem
3.1
:
See
3.1

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
E.1
, to prove the statement
of the theorem we have to show the equivalence of the constraints:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\quad Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)

(14)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\iff

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A},\forall\overline{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}:\quad Q^{\overline{\pi}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{\overline{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p,r),

(15)

where we have exchanged the order of the quantifiers (because they all are of the same type).
Observe that Eq.
14
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\quad Q^{\pi^{*}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{\pi^{*}}_{h}(s,a;p,r),

because of the existence of some optimal policy
\pi^{*}

(
Puterman 1994
, see)
.
Now, by induction over
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
, it is easy to show that Eq.
14
entails the existence of an optimal policy
\pi^{*}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
.
Therefore, we can rewrite the constraint as:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\quad Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{E}}}_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s);p,r)\geq Q^{\pi^{*}}_{h}(s,a;p,r),

since playing
\pi^{*}
from
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
brings again into
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
. By definition of
\pi^{*}
,
we have:

\displaystyle Q^{\pi^{*}}_{h}(s,a;p,r)

\displaystyle=Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\coloneqq\max\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}Q^{\pi}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\geq Q^{\overline{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p,r),

for all
\overline{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
. Since
\pi^{*}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
, then
we have shown that the two conditions in Eq.
14
and Eq.
15
are equivalent.
∎

As a direct consequence of Theorem
3.1
, we have the following
corollary.
See
D.1

Proof.

Let
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
and let
p
be a transition model in
\mathcal{P}
.
Define:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{a}\coloneqq\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,Q_{h}^{*}(s,a;p,r)=\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{\prime};p,r)\},

i.e., the set of rewards satisfying the constraint on the optimality of action
a

in a single
(s,h)
pair. It is well known
(
Puterman 1994
, see)
that,
given a reward function and a transition model, there always exists an optimal policy
whose Q-function coincides, by definition, with the optimal Q-function. In other words,
for any
p\in\mathcal{P}
and any
r\in\mathfrak{R}
, the optimal Q-function is “well-defined”.
Therefore, it holds that:

\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathfrak{R}_{a}=\mathfrak{R},

because
we are making the union of the rewards that induce action
a
to be optimal
in
(s,h)
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

To put it simple, if we add the constraint that at pair
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket

there
exists
an optimal action, we are not actually adding a constraint.
Notice that we can do the same with policies
\pi\in\Pi
instead of
actions
a\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thanks to Lemma
E.1
and the property
just highlighted, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}:\,Q_{h}^{*}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p,r)=\max\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\exists\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}:\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket:\,Q_{h}^{*}(s,\pi_{h}^{\prime}(s);p,r)=\max\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p,r)\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup\limits_{\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{\prime}}.

In this way, the constraints are defined only for
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
.
∎

With regards to Section
4
, we provide the following proposition.
See
4.1

Proof.

The first inequality is straightforward. For the second, observe that:

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(r,\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|,\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|,\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|,\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\bigl\{\sum\limits_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|,\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\max\bigl\{\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|,\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in[H]}\biggl(\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|+\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}|r_{h}(s,a)-\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)|\biggr)

\displaystyle=:\frac{1}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}d(r,\widehat{r}),

where at (1) we have upper bounded

\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}\leq\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)
for
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}
,
and
\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}\leq 1
, and
at (2) and (3) we have used that
\max\{a,b\}\leq a+b
for
a,b\geq 0
.
∎

Appendix F
Sample Complexity Analysis

In this section, we present our results on the sample complexity of
IRLO
(Algorithm
1
-
IRLO
box)
and
PIRLO
(Algorithm
1
-
PIRLO
box) with both distances
d
and
d_{\infty}
.

The section is organized into various subsections. We begin with Section
F.1
, in which we present the concentration results that will be used in all the sample complexity proofs. Section
F.2
contains the proofs of sample complexity of
IRLO
w.r.t.
distances
d
and
d_{\infty}
. Analogously, Section
F.3
contains the proofs of sample complexity of
PIRLO
w.r.t. distances
d
and
d_{\infty}
.
In Section
F.4
, we present additional sample complexity results for
PIRLO
w.r.t.
d,d_{\infty}
under additional requirements.
We conclude with Section
F.5
, in which we present a result of sample complexity on the estimation problem of the superset only, as defined in Equation (
8
).

F.1
Concentration Lemmas

We define
good event

\mathcal{E}
as the intersection of four events
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{4}
. Events
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}

allow to obtain exact estimates of
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
w.h.p., while events
\mathcal{E}_{3}
and
\mathcal{E}_{4}
allow to concentrate the estimates of the transition models around their means.

Lemma F.1

(Coverage Events)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP without reward and let

\pi^{E}
be the expert’s policy. Let

\mathcal{D}^{b}=\{\langle s_{h}^{b,i},a_{h}^{b,i}\rangle_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\}_{i\in\llbracket\tau^{b}\rrbracket}

and
\mathcal{D}^{E}=\{\langle s_{h}^{E,j},a_{h}^{E,j}\rangle_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\}_{j\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket^{E}}

be datasets of

\tau^{b}
and
\tau^{E}
trajectories collected
by executing policies
\pi^{b}
and
\pi^{E}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
Denote with
N_{h}^{b}(s,a)

the visitation count of triple
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}

computed using
\mathcal{D}^{b}
, and by
N_{h}^{E}(s,a)
the analogous for
\mathcal{D}^{E}
.
For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, define events
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Biggl\{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\geq 1,\;\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\quad\text{when }\tau^{b}\geq c_{1}\frac{\ln\frac{|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}}\Biggr\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Biggl\{N^{E}_{h}(s,a)\geq 1,\;\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}\quad\text{when }\tau^{E}\geq c_{2}\frac{\ln\frac{|\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}|}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{E},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}_{\min}}}\Biggr\},

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are universal constants. Then, event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds with
probability at least
1-\delta/2
.

Proof.

Let us begin with event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
. We observe that

N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\sim\text{Bin}(\tau^{b},\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a))
.
In an analogous manner as Lemma E.5 of
Metelli et al. 2023
,
we can write:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\complement})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}(\exists(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}:\;N_{h}^{b}(s,a)=0)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}(N_{h}^{b}(s,a)=0)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}(1-\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a))^{\tau^{b}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}(1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min})^{\tau^{b}}

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|(1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min})^{\tau^{b}}\leq\frac{\delta}{4},

where at (1) we used a union bound, and at (2) the definition of

\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)
.
Solving w.r.t.
\tau^{b}
we get:

\displaystyle\tau^{b}\geq\frac{\ln\frac{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}},

from which the bound for event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds for some uninteresting constant
c_{1}
.

Observe that, by following a similar reasoning, we can prove the bound for event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
,
by recalling that, by hypothesis, the expert’s policy is deterministic, so
|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}|=|\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}|
.
The statement of the theorem follows by an application of the union bound.
∎

Before presenting the next lemma, we introduce the symbols:

\displaystyle{b}_{h}(s,a)\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{2{\beta}(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta)}{\max\{N^{b}_{h}(s,a),1\}}},

(16)

and

\displaystyle{\beta}(n,\delta)\coloneqq\ln(4{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}/{\delta})+({S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}-1)\ln(e(1+n/{({S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}-1)})),

(17)

with
{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\coloneqq|{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}|
and
{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}\coloneqq\max_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}|{\mathcal{S}}_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}|
. The corresponding counterparts with the estimated quantities are given as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{h}(s,a)\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{2\widehat{\beta}(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta)}{\max\{N^{b}_{h}(s,a),1\}}},

(18)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}(n,\delta)\coloneqq\ln(4\widehat{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}/{\delta})+(\widehat{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}-1)\ln(e(1+n/{(\widehat{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}-1)})),

(19)

with
\widehat{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\coloneqq|\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}|
and
\widehat{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}\coloneqq\max_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}|
. Clearly, under the good event
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the two versions coincide.

Lemma F.2

(Concentration)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP without reward and let

\pi^{E}
be the expert’s policy. Let

\mathcal{D}^{b}=\{\langle s_{h}^{b,i},a_{h}^{b,i}\rangle_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\}_{i\in\llbracket\tau^{b}\rrbracket}

and
\mathcal{D}^{E}=\{\langle s_{h}^{E,j},a_{h}^{E,j}\rangle_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\}_{j\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket^{E}}

be datasets of

\tau^{b}
and
\tau^{E}
trajectories collected
by executing policies
\pi^{b}
and
\pi^{E}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
Denote with
\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
the empirical transition model of triple
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
computed using
\mathcal{D}^{b}
.
For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, define events
\mathcal{E}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{4}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Biggl\{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)KL(\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|p_{h}(\cdot|s,a))\leq\beta(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta),\quad\forall\tau^{b}\in\mathbb{N},\,\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\Biggr\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{4}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Biggl\{\frac{1}{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\vee 1}\leq c_{4}\frac{\ln\frac{|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\tau^{b}\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)},\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\Biggr\},

where
c_{4}
is a universal constant.
Then, event
\mathcal{E}_{3}\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}
holds with probability at least
1-\delta/2
.

Proof.

We show that both events
\mathcal{E}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{4}
hold with
probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{4}
. The thesis follows through the application
of a union bound.

Let us begin with
event
\mathcal{E}_{3}
.
Similarly to the proof of Lemma 10 in
Kaufmann et al. 2021
, we apply Lemma

J.2
and a union bound to get:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\complement})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}\biggl(\exists(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}},\,\exists\tau^{b}\in\mathbb{N}:\;N_{h}^{b}(s,a)KL(\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|p_{h}(\cdot|s,a))>\beta(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta)\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}\biggl(\exists\tau^{b}\in\mathbb{N}:\;N_{h}^{b}(s,a)KL(\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|p_{h}(\cdot|s,a))>\beta(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta)\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}\frac{\delta}{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}=\frac{\delta}{4}.

It should be remarked that, in the definition of
\beta
(Equation
17
),
we have used
|\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}|

instead of
\mathcal{S}
because it better represents the support of the transition model
in triples
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.

As far as event
\mathcal{E}_{4}
is concerned, consider an arbitrary triple
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.
Observe that the visitation count
N_{h}^{b}(s,a)

is binomially distributed, i.e.,

N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\sim\text{Bin}(\tau,\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a))
.
Therefore, similarly to Lemma B.1 of
(
Xie et al. 2021
)
,
by applying Lemma
J.1
with confidence

\delta/(4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|)
, we can concentrate the binomial as:

\frac{\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)}{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\vee 1}\leq\frac{8\ln\frac{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\tau},

from which we get:

\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\vee 1}\leq\frac{8\ln\frac{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\tau\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)}\Bigr)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}.

We can perform a union bound over
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
to get:

\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p,\pi^{b}}\Bigl(\exists(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}:\;\frac{1}{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)\vee 1}>\frac{8\ln\frac{4|\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}|}{\delta}}{\tau\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(s,a)}\Bigr)\leq\frac{\delta}{4}.

By choosing
c_{4}
appropriately, we get the result.
∎

Since
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{3}\cap\mathcal{E}_{4}
, then,
by combining Lemma
F.1
with Lemma
F.2
through a union bound, we get that
\mathcal{E}
holds w.p.
1-\delta
.

F.2
Proof of Theorem
5.1
and Theorem
5.2

The next lemmas show that, for any reward in
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
(
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
),
it is possible to find a “similar” reward in the estimate
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cap}
(
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}
).
Notice that, under events
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
we have that, respectively,

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}=\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}=\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
.
For the sake of simplicity, we provide here the (recursive) definitions of
p^{m},p^{M}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
for any
r\in\mathfrak{R}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}p^{M}&\coloneqq\begin{cases}p^{M}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=p_{h}(\cdot|s,a),\qquad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
p^{M}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot=\argmax\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r\},\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\\
p^{m}&\coloneqq\begin{cases}p^{m}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=p_{h}(\cdot|s,a),\qquad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
p^{m}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot=\argmin\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V^{\pi^{m}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{m},r\},\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\end{split}

(20)

where we have used the following (recursive) policy definitions
\pi^{M},\widetilde{\pi}^{m}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi^{M}&\coloneqq\begin{cases}\pi^{M}_{h}(s)=\pi^{E}_{h}(s),\;\text{if }(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}\\
\pi^{M}_{h}(\cdot|s)=\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot=\argmax\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r)\},\;\text{if }(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}\\
\end{cases},\\
\pi^{m}&\coloneqq\begin{cases}\pi^{m}_{h}(s)=\pi^{E}_{h}(s),\;\text{if }(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}\\
\pi^{m}_{h}(\cdot|s)=\mathbbm{1}\{\cdot={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\argmax\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}}Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r)\},\;\text{if }(s,h)\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}\\
\end{cases}.\end{split}

(21)

Thanks to these definitions, we can rewrite
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}&=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r)\geq Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r)\},\\
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}&=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r)\geq Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r)\}.\end{split}

(22)

We will denote by
\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{\pi}^{M},\widehat{\pi}^{m}

the transition models and policies defined as in Eq.
20
and Eq.
21
but for transition model
\widehat{p}
.

Lemma F.3

(Reward Choice Subset)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
be the subset of the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
estimated through

\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cap}
outputted by Algorithm
1
.
Under event
\mathcal{E}
, for any
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
,
the reward
\widehat{r}
constructed as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r)-\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r}),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\end{cases},

belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cap}
. Moreover, for any reward
\widehat{r}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cap}
, we can construct in the
same manner a reward
r
that belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is to show that
Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r)=Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})
for all
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
. Indeed, in this way, since
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
, then
it holds that:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widehat{p},\widehat{r})-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r)-Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r)\geq 0.

Let us begin with any
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
. By definition of
\widehat{r}
and by rearranging the terms, we obtain:

\displaystyle\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r)

\displaystyle\iff Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r).

(23)

In particular, observe that, by Assumption
2.1
, it holds
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
; moreover, by definition of
\widehat{p}^{M}
and
p^{M}
, playing an expert’s action from
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
brings again into
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, therefore, for
(s,a^{E},h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, this means:

\displaystyle Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widehat{p},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r).

Now, we show by induction that, for any
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
,
it holds that:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r).

As case base, consider stage
H
. Clearly, for any
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{H}
, we have:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{H}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h}(s,a;p^{M},r),

where we have used the definition of
\widehat{r}
.
Make the inductive hypothesis that, at stage
h+1
, for any

(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}
,
it holds that

Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h+1}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s,a;p^{M},r)
, and consider stage
h
:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}^{M}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{h}(s,a)}+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}^{M}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r}),\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{M}}}(s^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{M},r}),\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\max\bigl\{\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\pi^{M}}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r),\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{M}}}(s^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{M},r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}^{\pi^{M}}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r)

\displaystyle=Q_{h}^{\pi^{M}}(s,a;p^{M},r),

where at (1) we use the definition of
\hat{r}
outside
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
,
at (2) we use the definition of
\widehat{p}^{M}
,
at (3) we use Eq.
F.2
,
and at (4) we use the inductive hypothesis.

Notice that the same passages can be carried out if we exchanged
p
and
\widehat{p}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Notice that the reward function chosen in Lemma
F.3

can be interpreted, in an analogous manner as in the proof of Theorem 3.1 of
Metelli et al. 2021
,
as the reward that provides, in transition model
\widehat{p}
, the same Q-function provided by the given reward in
p
.

We can prove an analogous result for the superset
\mathcal{R}_{\widehat{p},\widehat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}
.

Lemma F.4

(Reward Choice Superset)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
be the subset of the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
estimated through

\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}
outputted by Algorithm
1
.
Under event
\mathcal{E}
, for any
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
,
the reward
\widehat{r}
constructed as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\pi^{m}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{m},r)-\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r}),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)=r_{h}(s,a),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\end{cases},

belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}
. Moreover, for any reward
\widehat{r}\in\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}
, we can construct in the
same manner a reward
r
that belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is analogous to that of Lemma
F.3
, and is reported here for completeness. We aim to show that
Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r)=Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})
for all
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
. Indeed, in this way, since
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
, then
it holds that:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widehat{p},\widehat{r})-Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r)-Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r)\geq 0.

Let us begin with any
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
. By definition of
\widehat{r}
and by rearranging the terms, we obtain:

\displaystyle\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{\pi^{m}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{m},r)

\displaystyle\iff Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r).

(24)

In particular, observe that, by Assumption
2.1
, it holds
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
; moreover, by definition of
\widehat{p}^{m}
and
p^{m}
, playing an expert’s action from
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
brings again into
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, therefore, for
(s,a^{E},h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, this means:

\displaystyle Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widehat{p},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,a^{E};p,r).

Now, we show by induction that, for any
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
,
it holds that:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r).

As case base, consider stage
H
. Clearly, for any
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{H}
, we have:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{H}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=r_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h}(s,a;p^{m},r),

where we have used the definition of
\widehat{r}
.
Make the inductive hypothesis that, at stage
h+1
, for any

(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}
,
it holds that

Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h+1}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})=Q^{\pi^{m}}_{h+1}(s,a;p^{m},r)
, and consider stage
h
:

\displaystyle Q^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}^{m}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{h}(s,a)}+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widehat{p}^{m}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\min\bigl\{\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r}),\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\min\bigl\{\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{m}}}(s^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{m},r}),\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\pi}^{m}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{m},\widehat{r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\min\bigl\{\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{\pi^{m}}(s^{\prime};p^{m},r),\min\limits_{s^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h+1}}V_{h+1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{m}}}(s^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p^{m},r})\bigr\}

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}^{\pi^{m}}(s^{\prime};p^{m},r)

\displaystyle=Q_{h}^{\pi^{m}}(s,a;p^{m},r),

where at (1) we use the definition of
\hat{r}
outside
\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
,
at (2) we use the definition of
\widehat{p}^{m}
,
at (3) we use Eq.
F.2
,
and at (4) we use the inductive hypothesis.

Notice that the same passages can be carried out if we exchanged
p
and
\widehat{p}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

From the proofs of Lemma
F.3
and Lemma

F.4
, we notice that proving the result for the superset is “easier”, because we simply have to consider a single transition model; instead, for the subset, we have to consider all the transition models in the equivalence
class. Luckily, we can single out a “worst” transition model from this class and
provide the proof only for it. We will see in the proofs of the results with pessimism
how to cope with the trickier problem in which there exist many “worst” transition models, and thus the recursion cannot be applied directly.

Thanks to Lemma
F.3
and Lemma
F.4
,
we can upper bound the distance between sets of rewards by a term that depends on the distance between the transition models. We do not have error for the policy because, under good event
\mathcal{E}
, we have that
\widehat{\pi}^{E}=\pi^{E}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}=\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
.

Lemma F.5

(Performance Decomposition Subset and Superset)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cap}

and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}\coloneqq\mathcal{R}_{\hat{p},\hat{\pi}^{E}}^{\cup}

be the output of IRLO (Algorithm
1
).
Under the good event
\mathcal{E}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{d}(\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap})\leq H\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}(\cdot,\cdot)}b_{h}(s,a),

and:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{d}(\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup})\leq H\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}(\cdot,\cdot)}b_{h}(s,a).

Proof.

By Definition
4.2
, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}\inf\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}d(r,\widehat{r}),\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}\inf\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}d(r,\widehat{r})\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\max\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}d(r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{r}^{1}}),\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}d({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{r}^{2}},\widehat{r})\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\max\Biggl\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\biggl(\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|r_{h}(s,a)-\widetilde{r}^{1}_{h}(s,a)\bigr|+\underbrace{\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}\bigl|r_{h}(s,a)-\widetilde{r}^{1}_{h}(s,a)\bigr|}_{=0}\biggr),

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\biggl(\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)-\widetilde{r}^{2}_{h}(s,a)\bigr|+\underbrace{\max\limits_{(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}}\bigl|\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)-\widetilde{r}^{2}_{h}(s,a)\bigr|}_{=0}\biggr)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}\max\biggl\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))V^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r)\bigr|,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\bigl|\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))V^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r})\bigr|\biggr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}\max\biggl\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bigl|}(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))V^{\pi^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{M},r){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bigr|},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}\frac{1}{M}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bigl|}(\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))V^{\widehat{\pi}^{M}}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widehat{p}^{M},\widehat{r}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bigr|}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}\max\biggl\{\sup\limits_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\bigl|(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}H}\bigr|,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\sup\limits_{\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\bigl|(\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}H}\bigr|\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\bigl|(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))H\bigr|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}H\bigl\|p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\bigr\|_{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(6)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}H\sqrt{2KL\bigl(p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\bigr)}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(7)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}H\sqrt{2\frac{\beta(N_{h}^{b}(s,a),\delta)}{N_{h}^{b}(s,a)}}

\displaystyle=H\sum\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket}\E\limits_{(s,a)\sim\rho_{h}^{p,\pi^{b}}(\cdot,\cdot)}b_{h}(s,a),

where at (1) we have applied Lemma
F.3
,
denoting by
\widetilde{r}^{1}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
and
\widetilde{r}^{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
the choices of rewards,
and used that
\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}f(x)\leq f(\bar{x})
for any
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}
;
at (2) we have used the definition of distance
d
,
at (3) we have inserted the definitions of
\widetilde{r}^{1}
and
