F.5
A note on the superset without relaxation

In this section, we show that, if we use the superset definition
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
of Eq.
8
, i.e., the definition without relaxation, then we are able to obtain the same performance decomposition result (see Lemma
F.5
) that we had for the case without pessimism, and, thus, we end up with the same sample complexity result, which is much smaller than those computed for the relaxations. Observe that we are not able to have an analogous result for the subset definition
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
of Eq.
8
.
Indeed, differently from the relaxations defined in Eq.
6
, the subset and the superset definitions of Eq.
8
are not exactly simmetric.
While
r\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
entails, by definition, the existence of (at least) one transition model in
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)
in which
r
induces an optimal policy
\pi^{*}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
, this is not true for
r^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
.
Indeed, potentially, there might exist a (worst)
18
18

18

Worst because there is a universal quantifier
\forall
over transition models in the definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
.
transition model for every
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
. Therefore, intuitively, in the reward choice lemma, we cannot make a choice of a single (worst) transition model, but we have to choose many of them. This fact “breaks” the recursion and it does not allow us to perform
\ell_{1}
-norm bounds.
Instead, since the superset is of different nature, we can.
It should be remarked that a membership checker algorithm for superset
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
is inefficient to implement in practice because it requires to solve a bilinear optimization problem (see Appendix
G
).

We will denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
the superset definition of Eq.
8
.

Lemma F.20

(Reward Choice)
.

Under good event
\mathcal{E}
,
for any
\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
,
the reward
r
constructed as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}r_{h}(s,a)=\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(\widecheck{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};\widecheck{p},\widehat{r}),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
r_{h}(s,a)=\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\end{cases},

where
\widecheck{p}
is some transition model in
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)
,
belongs to
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
,
we have that

\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
if and only if there exists a transition model
\bar{p}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)

such that
\widehat{r}\in\mathcal{R}^{\cup}_{\bar{p},\pi^{E}}
. Let us construct

r
by choosing
\widecheck{p}=\bar{p}
. To show that
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
,
we are going to show that the transition model
\widetilde{p}

defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widetilde{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=p_{h}(\cdot|s,a),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\widetilde{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\bar{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a),\quad\forall(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}\\
\end{cases},

belongs to
[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}}
and is such that, for all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, for all
a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}
:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r)\leq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widetilde{p},r).

Then, by Lemma
E.1
, we can conclude that
r\in\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
.

Trivially, notice that
\widetilde{p}\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}p
.
We proceed by induction to show that, for all
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\bar{p},\widehat{r})=Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r).

Then, since
\widehat{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
, for all
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
and for all
a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}
,
the inequality

Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\bar{p},\widehat{r})\leq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{E};\bar{p},\widehat{r})
(Lemma
E.1
) entails

Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r)\leq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{E};\widetilde{p},r)
, and the thesis follows.

As case base, consider stage
H
. For any
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, thanks to the definition of
r
,
we can write:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{H}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r)

\displaystyle=r_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\widehat{r}_{H}(s,a)

\displaystyle=Q^{*}_{H}(s,a;\bar{p},\widehat{r}).

Now, make the inductive hypothesis that for all
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, it holds that

Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\widetilde{p},r)=Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\bar{p},\widehat{r})
, and consider stage
h
.
For any
(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}
, we can write:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\widetilde{p},r)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{p},\widehat{r}})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}(\widecheck{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a))\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\widecheck{p},\widehat{r})+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\bar{p},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{=}\widehat{r}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\bar{p},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{=}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\bar{p},\widehat{r}),

where at (1) we have applied the Bellman’s optimality equation, at (2) we have used the inductive hypothesis,
at (3) we have inserted the definition of
r_{h}(s,a)
along with the fact that
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
,
at (4) we have noticed that
\widecheck{p}=\bar{p}
(by choice) and that
\widetilde{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)

(by definition); finally, at (5), we have applied again the Bellman’s optimality equation.

On the other side, for any
(s,a)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}_{h}
, we can write:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\widetilde{p},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\widetilde{p},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{p},\widehat{r}})

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{r}}_{h}(s,a)+\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bar{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\bar{p},\widehat{r})

\displaystyle=Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;\bar{p},\widehat{r}),

where at (1) we have used both the definition of
r_{h}(s,a)

and that
\widetilde{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)=\bar{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
, for
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Thanks to the reward choice lemma just presented, we obtain the following sample complexity result.

Theorem F.21
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP without reward and let

\pi^{E}
be the expert’s policy. Let

\mathcal{D}^{E}
and
\mathcal{D}^{b}
be two datasets of

\tau^{E}
and
\tau^{b}
trajectories collected
with policies
\pi^{E}
and
\pi^{b}
in
\mathcal{M}
, respectively.
Under Assumption
2.1
, any algorithm
\mathfrak{A}
that outputs
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
(defined as in Eq.
8
) is such that, for any
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}_{(p,\pi^{E},\pi^{b})}\bigl(\bigl\{\mathcal{H}_{c}(\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup})\leq\epsilon\bigr\}\wedge\{\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}\}\bigr)\geq 1-\delta,

with a sample complexity at most:

\displaystyle\tau^{b}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Bigg(\frac{H^{3}Z^{p,\pi^{b}}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+S^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}\biggr)+\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\tau^{E}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Bigg(\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{E},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}_{\min}}}\Bigg).

if
c=d
, and a sample complexity at most:

\displaystyle\tau^{b}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Bigg(\frac{H^{4}\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+S^{p,\pi^{b}}_{\max}\biggr)+\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{b},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{b}}}_{\min}}}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\tau^{E}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Bigg(\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{\ln\frac{1}{1-\rho^{\pi^{E},\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}_{\min}}}\Bigg).

if
c=d_{\infty}
.

Proof Sketch.

Observe that, thanks to Lemma
F.20
, we are able to obtain a performance decomposition lemma analogous to Lemma
F.5
for distance
d
(or analogous for distance
d_{\infty}
). Next, following the steps in the proof of Theorem
5.1
(Theorem
5.2
), we obtain the result.
∎

Appendix G
Implementation

In this appendix, we provide some comments on the implementation of membership checker algorithms for
IRLO
,
PIRLO
, and some comments about the subset and superset defined in Eq.
8
. In Section
G.1
, we present the pseudocode of the membership checker algorithms. In Section

G.2
, we provide more details on the definitions of the relaxations
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
.
In Section
G.3
we show that the implementation of a membership checker algorithm for the subset and superset defined in Eq.
8
is inefficient. Finally, in Section
G.4
, we give the intuition that a straightforward relaxation of the representation of the sets provided by Lemma
E.1
is worse than that obtained by relaxing the representation in Theorem
3.1
.

G.1
Algorithm

The pseudocode of the membership checker algorithms for
IRLO
and
PIRLO
is provided in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

Membership checker for
IRLO
and
PIRLO
.

Input :

Datasets
\mathcal{D}^{E}=\{\langle s_{h}^{E,i},a_{h}^{E,i}\rangle_{h}\}_{i}
,
\mathcal{D}^{b}=\{\langle s_{h}^{b,i},a_{h}^{b,i}\rangle_{h}\}_{i}
, candidate reward function
r\in\mathfrak{R}

Output :

\mathsf{True}
if
r\in(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup},\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap})

Run lines
1
-
1
of Algorithm
1

Define
\mathcal{C}
as in Eq. (
6
) for
IRLO
and as in Eq. (
7
) for
PIRLO

Extended value iteration:

\mathcal{A}_{h}(s)\leftarrow\text{{ if }}(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}\text{{ then }}\{\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s)\}\text{ {else} }\mathcal{A},\;\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket

Q_{H}^{+}(s,a),Q_{H}^{-}(s,a)\leftarrow r_{H}(s,a)

\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket

for

h=H-1

to

1

do

for

(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}

do

Q_{h}^{+}(s,a)\leftarrow r_{h}(s,a)+\max\limits_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{p}^{\prime}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{h+1}(s)}Q_{h+1}^{+}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

Q_{h}^{-}(s,a)\leftarrow r_{h}(s,a)+\min\limits_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}{p}^{\prime}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\max\limits_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{h+1}(s)}Q_{h+1}^{-}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

end for

end for

Membership test:

in
{}^{\cup}\leftarrow\mathsf{True}
, in
{}^{\cap}\leftarrow\mathsf{True}

for

(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}

do

for

a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{\widehat{\pi}^{E}(s)\}

do

IRLO

if

Q_{h}^{+}(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}^{E}(s))<Q_{h}^{+}(s,a)

then

in
{}^{\cup}\leftarrow\mathsf{False}

end if

if

Q_{h}^{-}(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}^{E}(s))<Q_{h}^{-}(s,a)

then

in
{}^{\cap}\leftarrow\mathsf{False}

end if

PIRLO

if

Q_{h}^{+}(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}^{E}(s))<Q_{h}^{-}(s,a)

then

in
{}^{\cup}\leftarrow\mathsf{False}

else if

Q_{h}^{-}(s,\widehat{\pi}_{h}^{E}(s))<Q_{h}^{+}(s,a)

then

in
{}^{\cap}\leftarrow\mathsf{False}

end if

end for

end for

return
(in
∪
, in
∩
)

The idea is to find the worst (resp. best) transition model for the subset (resp. superset) among all those feasible. In practice, what we do is to exploit the representation provided in Eq.
22
for
IRLO
and the representation provided in Eq.
27
for
PIRLO
.

Observe that, inside the support
(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, we use the estimated expert’s action
\widehat{\pi}^{E}_{h}(s)
, while outside the support we always play the action that maximizes the Q-function (both
Q^{+}
and
Q^{-}
). Concerning the transition model, notice that, for
Q^{+}
, we consider the
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
that maximizes the expected
Q^{+}
, while for
Q^{-}
we consider the
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
that minimizes the expected
Q^{-}
. Observe also that, for
IRLO
, because of the definition of
\mathcal{C}
, inside the support
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}
we use
p^{\prime}=\widehat{p}
, and outside the support we consider
p^{\prime}=\argmax_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}
for
Q^{+}
and
p^{\prime}=\argmin_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}
for
Q^{-}
.

Finally, we check the Bellman optimality conditions to assess the membership of the candidate reward
r
in the estimated sets
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
(boolean variable in
∪
) and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
(boolean variable in
∩
) (line
2
).

G.2
A better understanding of the relaxations

To get a better understanding of why
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
, under the hypothesis (good event) that the true transition model
p\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)
and that
\widehat{\pi}^{E}=\pi^{E}
in all
\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, observe that, for the subset:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}

\displaystyle\supseteq\bigcap\limits_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{E}}^{\cap}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b),\forall\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}},\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\supseteq}\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}},\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\min_{\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\Bigl(\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)-\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\Bigr)\geq 0\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap},

where at (1) we have exchanged the order of the quantifiers, and we can do so because they all are of the same type, and then we have observed that
\min_{x}(f(x)-g(x))\geq\min_{x}f(x)-\max_{x}g(x)
, and at (2) we recognize that the first term does not depend on
\bar{\pi}
.
W.r.t. the superset, in an analogous manner, observe that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\mathcal{R}_{p^{\prime},\pi^{E}}^{\cup}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\exists p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b),\forall\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}},\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\subseteq}\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}},\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle=\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\min_{\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\Bigl(\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)-\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\Bigr)\geq 0\}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\{r\in\mathfrak{R}\,|\,\forall(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}:\;\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\pi^{E}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{\bar{\pi}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{\bar{\pi}}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup},

where at (1) we have relaxed and at (2) we recognize that the first term does not depend on
\bar{\pi}
.

G.3
Testing the membership without relaxations

We show that the problem of testing the membership to the
subset and superset defined as in Eq.
8

is equivalent to solving a bilinear optimization problem, which is in general hard.
We will denote the expert’s action by
a^{E}
and we use the representation provided by Lemma
E.1
.

Let us begin with the subset
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
.
A given reward
r
belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\forall p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b),\forall(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\leq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{E};p^{\prime},r),

where
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)
is defined in Eq.
7
.
By applying the Bellman optimality equation, changing the order of the quantifiers,
and considering the worst possible transition model, we obtain:

\displaystyle\forall(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:

\displaystyle r_{h}(s,a)\leq r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\min\limits_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\biggl(\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a^{E})V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)-\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{h}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a)V^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)\biggr).

It should be remarked that, differently from
IRLO
and
PIRLO
,
to check whether a given reward
r
belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
,
we cannot optimize the value function, but we have to optimize the advantage function.
Therefore, for all
(\bar{s},\bar{h})\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, and for all
\bar{a}\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}
,
we have to solve the optimization problem:

\displaystyle\min\limits_{p^{\prime}}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Bigl(p_{\bar{h}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|\bar{s},a^{E})-p_{\bar{h}}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|\bar{s},\bar{a})\Bigr)V^{*}_{\bar{h}+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }\|p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{1}\leq b_{h}(s,a)\quad\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{b}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\;p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{S}}\quad\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket

\displaystyle\quad\quad\;p_{h}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=0\quad\quad\forall(s,a,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{p,\pi^{E}}\wedge s^{\prime}\notin\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}}_{h+1}\cup\text{supp }\widehat{p}_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})\big).

Observe that, while the set of constraints define a convex set, the objective involves the product of optimization variables. We can introduce
H
variables
\{V_{s}\}_{s\in\llbracket S\rrbracket}
to replace
V^{*}_{\bar{h}+1}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)
by adding suitable constraints that keep into account also the presence of the maximum operator inside
V^{*}
(because of the Bellman’s optimality equation).
In this way, due to the product between variables
p^{\prime}
and
V
, we can conclude that this is a bilinear optimization problem, which is in general difficult to solve.

W.r.t. the superset, we have that a reward
r
belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\exists p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b),\forall(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}:\;Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)\leq Q^{*}_{h}(s,a^{E};p^{\prime},r).

This time, we cannot bring the transition model inside because we have different quantifiers.
We can formulate the problem as a feasibility problem by adding constraints because of the presence of
\forall(s,h)\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{p,\pi^{E}},\forall a\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a^{E}\}
. In practice, the presence of the product between “optimization” variables is now in the constraints, so the problem is again a bilinear problem.

G.4
Relaxing the representation provided by Lemma
E.1

We have seen that we can represent the feasible set by using Theorem
3.1
or Lemma
E.1
. While the two representations are equivalent, observe that a straightforward relaxation of the constraints present in Lemma
E.1
provides a different relaxation of the subset and superset w.r.t.
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
(which are obtained by relaxing the representation in Theorem
3.1
).
Indeed, by relaxing the representation with the
Q^{*}
(Lemma
E.1
), we would obtain constraints of the form (ex. subset):

\displaystyle\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{*}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{*}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle\iff\quad\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\max\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}Q^{\pi}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\max\limits_{\pi\in\Pi}Q^{\pi}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r).

(50)

Clearly, this is different from
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
, whose constraints can be written as:

\min_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi^{E}}}_{h}(s,\pi_{h}^{E}(s);p^{\prime},r)\geq\max_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{p},b)}\max\limits_{\pi\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}}Q^{\pi}_{h}(s,a;p^{\prime},r).

Indeed,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
puts the additional constraint that the
Q^{*}
is achieved by a policy in
[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
, which is not present in Eq.
G.4
.

An analogous reasoning can be carried out also for the superset.

Appendix H
Proofs of Section
8

In this section, we provide the missing proofs of Section
8
.

See
8.1

Proof.

Let
r
be an arbitrary reward function of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
.
Consider a certain
(s,h)\in\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, with expert’s action
\pi^{E}_{h}(s)=a^{E}
, and
let
a\in\mathcal{A}
be a non-expert’s action.
By Lemma
E.1
,
we know that, for any
p^{\prime}\in[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
, it must hold:

\displaystyle r_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)-\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p,r)-\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r),

where we have used the definition of
[p]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}
.
Since
(s,a,h)\notin\mathcal{Z}^{p,\pi^{E}}
, then the constraint must
hold
\forall p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular,
it must hold for the transition model such that:

\displaystyle r_{h}(s,a)\leq r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\underbrace{\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p,r)-\max\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)}_{\leq 0},

from which the thesis follows.
∎

See
8.2

Proof.

Let
a^{E}\coloneqq\pi^{E}_{h}(s)
.
Similarly to the proof of Proposition
8.1
,
we can write:

p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
:

\displaystyle r_{h}(s,a)

\displaystyle\leq r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)-\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}^{\prime}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=r_{h}(s,a^{E})+\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p,r)-\E\limits_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r),

where we have used that we have access to samples about
p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)
.
By hypothesis,
p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)\neq p_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})
, therefore,
by taking
r
such that

\E_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a^{E})}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p,r)>\E_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}V_{h+1}^{*}(s^{\prime};p^{\prime},r)
, we can obtain a reward
r
in
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}

such that

r_{h}(s,\pi^{E}_{h}(s))<r_{h}(s,a)
.

∎

Appendix I
A relaxed triangle inequality

In this section, we show that both our notions of distance
d,d_{\infty}
, defined in Section
4
, are semimetrics, and that they satisfy a
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality
(
Fagin & Stockmeyer 1998
, see)
with finite
\rho>1
for any pair of rewards
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{R}
.
Furthermore, we show that the Hausdorff distance
\mathcal{H}
, when applied to the sets of rewards considered in this work, inherits the relaxed triangle inequality property. It should be remarked that we need the
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality property with finite
\rho
just for the
learnability
proofs of Appendix
C
. Moreover, notice that we do not care about a tight value of
\rho
, but only that it is finite. Instead, if we wanted to compute a minimax lower bound, then we would need a tight value of
\rho
in order to obtain a tight lower bound.

I.1
d
and
d_{\infty}
satisfy a relaxed triangle inequality

In the following, for the sake of simplicity, we denote reward functions by vectors
x,y,z,\dotsc\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Moreover,
for any pair
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we will consider distance
d
for some distribution
q\in\Delta^{\llbracket k\rrbracket}
as:

\displaystyle d(x,y)=\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}q_{i}|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}},

and distance
d_{\infty}
as:

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(x,y)=\frac{\|x-y\|_{\infty}}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}.

First of all, let us see that neither
d
nor
d_{\infty}
are metrics:

Proposition I.1
.

Both the functions
d
and
d_{\infty}
do not satisfy the triangle inequality.

Proof.

To show that the triangle inequality property is not satisfied, we simply provide some counterexamples.
For the sake of simplicity, let
k=2
.

W.r.t. distance
d
, let the vectors
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=[1,0]^{\intercal}\\
y=[-1,-1]^{\intercal}\\
z=[-2,-1]^{\intercal}\end{cases},

and observe that, for any
q\in\Delta^{\llbracket 2\rrbracket}
such that
q_{2}>0
:

\displaystyle d(x,y)=2q_{1}+q_{2}\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{?}}}{\leq}d(x,z)+d(y,z)=3/2q_{1}+q_{2}/2+q_{1}/2=2q_{1}+q_{2}/2

\displaystyle\iff\,q_{2}\leq 0.

If
q_{2}=0
, we can take the second component of
z
to be arbitrary large so that the inequality is not verified.

As far as
d_{\infty}
is concerned,
let
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the vectors defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=[0,2]^{\intercal}\\
y=[2,2]^{\intercal}\\
z=[1,3]^{\intercal}\end{cases}.

We have:

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(x,y)=\frac{2}{2}=1\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{?}}}{\leq}d_{\infty}(x,z)+d_{\infty}(y,z)=1/3+1/3=2/3

\displaystyle\,\iff 1\leq 2/3,

which is clearly false.

Notice that it is possible to generate counterexamples for higher dimensions
k>2

by using a simple script of code.
∎

Having verified that distances
d,d_{\infty}
do not satisfy the triangle inequality, we can conclude that they are
semimetrics
, as it is easy to check the other three properties of positivity, simmetry, and that the distance between two points is zero if and only if the two points coincide. We are interested in verifying whether
they satisfy a relaxed form of triangle inequality
(
Fagin & Stockmeyer 1998
)
.
Specifically, for any finite
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
with
\rho>1
, we say that a function
d
satisfies
the
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality if, for any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
:

\displaystyle d(x,y)\leq\rho\bigl(d(x,z)+d(y,z)\bigr).

We aim to show that both
d
and
d_{\infty}
satisfy the
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality for some
\rho
.
Let us begin with a useful lemma.

Lemma I.2
.

Let
d_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the function that, for any pair
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, it returns:

\displaystyle d_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\|x-y\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2}\}}.

Then,
d_{2}
is a metric.

Proof.

It is easy to observe that
d_{2}(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
. Moreover, notice that
d_{2}(x,y)\geq 0
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and also that
d_{2}(x,y)=d_{2}(y,x)
.

It remains to prove that
d_{2}
satisfies the triangle inequality property, i.e., for any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, it satisfies:

\displaystyle d_{2}(x,y)\leq d_{2}(x,z)+d_{2}(y,z).

We distinguish two cases, one in which
\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}\neq\|z\|_{2}
and the other in which
\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}=\|z\|_{2}
.

Let us begin with the former case. W.l.o.g., assume that
\argmax\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}=y
. Then, we can write:

\displaystyle d_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\|x-y\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|x-y\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\|z\|_{2}\}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}+\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}+\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\max\{\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{\leq}\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}}+\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon d_{2}(x,z)+d_{2}(y,z),

where at (1) we apply triangle inequality of the
\|\cdot\|_{2}
norm,
at (2) we use that
\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}=\max\{\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}=\|y\|_{2}
, at (3) we use that, since

\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}=\|y\|_{2}
, then
\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}\geq\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}
.

Now, w.l.o.g., consider the case in which
\|x\|_{2}\leq\|y\|_{2}\leq\|z\|_{2}
. Since the normed vector space
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is an inner product space, then the Ptolemy’s inequality
(
Steele 2004
)
holds:

\displaystyle\|x-y\|_{2}\|z\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x-z\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}+\|y-z\|_{2}\|x\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|x-z\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}+\|y-z\|_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\|y\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\|y\|_{2}\bigl(\|x-z\|_{2}+\|y-z\|_{2}\bigr).

By dividing both sides of the inequality by
\|z\|_{2}
and
\|y\|_{2}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\frac{\|x-y\|_{2}}{\|y\|_{2}}\leq\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\|z\|_{2}}+\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\|z\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\|x-y\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2}\}}\leq\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}+\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

It should be remarked that the Ptolemy’s inequality holds in inner product spaces only, and that the unique
p
-normed vector space to be an inner product space is that with
p=2
. This is why our proof of Lemma
I.2
works for function
d_{p}
defined as:

\displaystyle d_{p}(x,y)\coloneqq\frac{\|x-y\|_{p}}{\max\{\|x\|_{p},\|y\|_{p}\}},

if and only if
p=2
.
Thanks to Lemma
I.2
, we are able to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem I.3
.

Let
q\in\Delta^{\llbracket k\rrbracket}
such that
q_{i}>0
for all
i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket
, and denote

q_{\min}\coloneqq\min_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}q_{i}
.
Then, both the semimetrics
d
and
d_{\infty}
satisfy
the
\rho
-relaxed triangle inequality with
\rho
upper bounded, respectively, by
k/q_{\min}^{2}
and
k
.

Proof.

First, we prove the statement of the theorem for
d_{\infty}
, and then we use it to prove the statement for
d
.

Observe that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
:

\displaystyle\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\|x\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}\|x\|_{\infty}.

(51)

Let us consider any three vectors
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
If
\argmax\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty},\|z\|_{\infty}\}\neq z
, then we can proceed as in the first part of the proof of Lemma
I.2
to show that

d_{\infty}(x,y)\leq d_{\infty}(x,z)+d_{\infty}(y,z)
.
Therefore, w.l.o.g., we consider the case in which
\argmax\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty},\|z\|_{\infty}\}=z
.
We can write:

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|x-y\|_{\infty}}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\frac{\|x-y\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}\frac{\sqrt{k}\|x-y\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}},\|y\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}\}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k}d_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{\leq}\sqrt{k}d_{2}(x,z)+\sqrt{k}d_{2}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{k}\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}+\sqrt{k}\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|y\|_{2},\|z\|_{2}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}\sqrt{k}\frac{\|x-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|x\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}},\|z\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}}\}}+\sqrt{k}\frac{\|y-z\|_{2}}{\max\{\|y\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}},\|z\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}}\}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}k}\frac{\|x-z\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}}}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|z\|_{\infty}\}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}k}\frac{\|y-z\|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\infty}}}{\max\{\|y\|_{\infty},\|z\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle=k\bigl(d_{\infty}(x,z)+d_{\infty}(y,z)\bigr),

where at (1) and at (2) we use Eq.
51
,
at (3) we use the result in Lemma
I.2
,
and at (4) and at (5) we use again Eq.
51
.

Now, we move to prove the statement concerning
d
.
In a similar way as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
, we have that, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
:

\displaystyle d(x,y)\leq d_{\infty}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\|x-y\|_{\infty}}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}\frac{q_{i}}{q_{i}}|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max\limits_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}\frac{q_{i}}{q_{\min}}|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket}q_{i}|x_{i}-y_{i}|}{q_{\min}\max\{\|x\|_{\infty},\|y\|_{\infty}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d(x,y)}{q_{\min}}.

By using this relation in place of that in Eq.
51
, we can carry out the
same derivation made for
d_{\infty}
using
d_{2}
for the semimetric
d
using
d_{\infty}
.
∎

It should be remarked that we are not claiming here that the values of
\rho
provided in Theorem
I.3
are tight
19
19

19

Indeed, we do not believe so. By using a script to generate a large number of vectors, and using the intuition
that the diagonal of the unit square (
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
) is
\sqrt{2}
the radius of the unit circle (
\|\cdot\|_{2}
),
we conjecture that a tighter value of
\rho
for
d_{\infty}
is
\rho=2
, irrespective of the dimension.

.

I.2
The Hausdorff distance inherits the relaxed triangle inequality property

First, we show that, thanks to the definitions of
d
and
d_{\infty}
, if we apply the Hausdorff distance to
closed
sets, then the (relaxed) triangle inequality property is satisfied. Next, we show that the sets of rewards we work with are
closed
.

Let us begin with the following proposition.

Proposition I.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{d}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
be defined as in Section
4
. The
closedness
of the sets to which these distances are applied is a sufficient condition for the (relaxed) triangle inequality property to hold.

Proof Sketch.

We will not provide an exhaustive proof, since it is completely analogous to the proof that shows that
compactness
is a sufficient condition for the Hausdorff distance with inner metric to satisfy triangle inequality. Instead, we simply give an idea of why for
d
and
d_{\infty}

closedness
(instead of
compactness
) suffices.

In practice, the compactness requirement is just needed to guarantee that the
infimum
is actually a
minimum
over the sets in input to the Hausdorff distance. For a generic notion of inner distance, closedness is not sufficient because the infimum might be at
\infty
and, thus, the minimum would not exist. However, observe that both
d
and
d_{\infty}
contain the normalization term
1/M
(see Section
4
), therefore, for any finite vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, getting to infinity

\lim_{y\to\infty}\|x-y\|_{\infty}/M=1
worsens the distance to
x
w.r.t. any other finite
z
in the set containing
y
. This shows that boundedness is not required anymore, but closedness suffices. This concludes the proof.
∎

In this work we consider unbounded sets of rewards, so clearly compactness does not hold. The following proposition shows the closedness of some sets of rewards.

Proposition I.5
.

The following sets are closed:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}},\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}},\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap},\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup},\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap},\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}.

Proof.

From Theorem 3 of
Ng & Russell 2000
, we observe that the old feasible set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{E}}
is closed because it is defined by linear less than or
equal to

\leq
inequalities.

The new feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
can be expressed, from Corollary
D.1
, as an arbitrary union of closed sets
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}=\bigcup_{\pi^{\prime}\in[\pi^{E}]_{\equiv_{\mathcal{S}^{p,\pi^{E}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{\prime}}
. However, observe that the feasible sets
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{p,\pi^{\prime}}
with stochastic
\pi^{\prime}
are contained in the feasible sets of some deterministic policies. Since there is a finite number of deterministic policies, then
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
can be expressed as a finite union of closed sets, so it is closed.

The subset
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cap}
is an arbitrary intersection of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, i.e., closed sets, thus it is closed.

The superset
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
is an arbitrary union of
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}
, so, potentially, it might not be non-closed. However, thanks to the definitions of
p^{m}
and
\pi^{m}
in Eq.
20
and Eq.
21
, we know that the arbitrary union representing
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
coincides with the feasible set
\mathcal{R}_{p^{m},\pi^{m}}
, which is closed, thus
\mathcal{R}_{p,\pi^{E}}^{\cup}
is closed.

In an analogous manner, by using Eq.
25
and Eq.
26
, we observe that the relaxations
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cap}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\cup}
can be expressed by a finite number of linear less than or
equal to

\leq
constraints, thus they are closed.
∎

Appendix J
Technical Lemmas

In this section, we report some technical lemmas that are useful in the analysis of the sample complexity
of
IRLO
and
PIRLO
(see Appendix
F
).
Lemma
J.1
and Lemma
J.2
are taken from
other works, while Lemma
J.3
takes inspiration from Lemma B.9 of
Metelli et al. 2021
.

Lemma J.1

(Lemma A.1 of
(
Xie et al. 2021
)
)
.

Suppose that
N\sim\text{Bin}(n,p)
is a binomially distributed random variable,
with
n\geq 1
and
p\in[0,1]
. Then, with probability
at least
1-\delta
, we have that:

\frac{p}{N\vee 1}\leq\frac{8\ln\frac{1}{\delta}}{n}.

Lemma J.2

(Lemma 8 of
(
Kaufmann et al. 2021
)
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\dotsc,X_{n},\dotsc
be i.i.d. samples from a distribution supported over

\llbracket m\rrbracket
, of probabilities given by
p\in\Delta^{\llbracket m\rrbracket}
. We denote by
\widehat{p}_{n}

the empirical vector of probabilities, i.e., for all
k\in\llbracket m\rrbracket
:

\widehat{p}_{n,k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{l=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}\{X_{l}=k\}.

For all
p\in\Delta^{\llbracket m\rrbracket}
, for all
\delta\in[0,1]
:

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\,nKL(\widehat{p}_{n}\|p)>\ln(1/\delta)+(m-1)\ln\bigl(e(1+n/(m-1))\bigr)\biggr)\leq\delta.

Lemma J.3
.

Let
a,b,c,d>0
such that
2bc>e
. Then, the inequality
x\geq a+b\ln(cx+d)
is satisfied
by all
x\geq 2a+3b\ln(2bc)+d/c
.

Proof.

Observe that, since function
x
grows faster than function
a+b\ln(cx+d)
,
then there exists
\bar{x}
such that, for all
x\geq\bar{x}
,
the inequality is satisfied. Our goal here is to show that
such
\bar{x}
can be upper bounded by
2a+3b\ln(2bc)+d/c
.

Let us consider any
x\geq 2a+d/c
. We can write:

\displaystyle x\geq a+b\ln(cx+d)\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{x-a}{b}\geq\ln(c(x\pm a)+d)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad e^{\frac{x-a}{b}}\geq c(x-a)+ca+d

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\Longleftarrow}\quad e^{\frac{x-a}{b}}\geq 2c(x-a)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{a-x}{b}e^{\frac{a-x}{b}}\geq-\frac{1}{2bc},

(I)

where at (1) we have used that, since
x\geq 2a+d/c
,
then
c(x-a)\geq ca+d
, and thus we have replaced the constraint with a stronger one.

Hypothesis
2bc>e
entails that
-\frac{1}{2bc}\geq-\frac{1}{e}
, thus we can apply the
Lambert function, which provides as solution to inequality
I
all the
x
such that:

\frac{a-x}{b}\leq W_{-1}\biggl(-\frac{1}{2bc}\biggr)\quad\text{ or }\quad\frac{a-x}{b}\geq W_{0}\biggl(-\frac{1}{2bc}\biggr),

where
W_{0}
is the principal component of the Lambert W function.
Consider the first inequality. We can write:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\geq a-bW_{-1}\biggl(-\frac{1}{2bc}\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}a+b+b\sqrt{2\ln(2bc)-2}+b\ln(2bc)-b

\displaystyle\leq a+3b\ln(2bc),

where at (1) we have applied the inequality
W_{-1}(-e^{-u-1})\geq-1-\sqrt{2u}-u

from
(
Chatzigeorgiou 2013
)
.

To obtain the result, we use that
\max\{a,b\}\leq a+b
for any
a,b\geq 0
to upper bound:

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{2a+d/c,a+3b\ln(2bc)\bigr\}=a+\max\bigl\{a+d/c,3b\ln(2bc)\bigr\}\leq 2a+3b\ln(2bc)+d/c.

∎

Appendix K
Illustrative Experiment

We have applied
PIRLO
to the highway driving application domain.
To this aim, we have used the data
20
20

20

The data is publicly available at
https://github.com/amarildolikmeta/irl_real_life/tree/main/datasets/highway
.

gathered by
Likmeta et al. 2021
.

Data Description

The dataset consists of trajectories of
H=400
stages collected by
10
different human experts driving in a simulator. The highway has 3 lanes. The goal of each expert is to change lane in order to drive safely and to minimize the trip time. The action space
\mathcal{A}
is made of 3 actions: Turn left, turn right, continue forward. The state space
\mathcal{S}
is continue, and it is represented by
25
features, keeping into account the speed and position of the car, and the speed and position of the surrounding cars.

Data Preprocessing

We have to transform the data to obtain a tabular MDP. To this aim, we construct
5
discrete features from the
25
present in the original data: We use three binary features, free left, free right, free forward, that say whether there is a vehicle on the left, on the right, or in front of our car; next, we use a binary feature that says whether the car is changing lane, and a discrete feature with
5
possible values for the speed of the vehicle. In this way, we obtain a tabular MDP with
S=80
.

Experiments Design

As mentioned by
Likmeta et al. 2021
, this lane-change scenario represents a multi-objective task, because humans consider several objectives while driving. We manually design some reward functions coherent with the most common driving objectives and we use
PIRLO
to verify whether they are compatible w.h.p. with the demonstrations of behavior provided by the 10 experts in the dataset.
First, we construct a single behavioral dataset
\mathcal{D}^{b}
by joining the trajectories of all the 10 experts, and then we consider one expert at a time to construct
\mathcal{D}^{E}
.
Next, we design the reward functions and we give them in input to the membership checker implementation of
PIRLO
.

Experiments Results

We design 3 kinds of reward functions:

•

reward
r_{\text{BC}}
, i.e., the “behavioral cloning” reward, which is the reward that assigns positive values to actions played by the expert’s policy;

•

reward
r
, which is coherent with the observations provided in Section 5.3 of
Likmeta et al. 2021
. In words, it assigns negative reward when (
i
) the right lane is not free, (
ii
) there is a car in front of us (and so it decreases our speed), (
iii
) we change lane;

•

reward
\overline{r}
, which is
-r
, i.e., it assigns positive reward to all the bad actions;

We provide the output of
PIRLO
in Table
2
.

Table 2:
The output of
PIRLO
when fed with the rewards designed for the highway driving task. The first letter refers to the superset, while the second letter refers to the subset. “N” means that the reward does not belong to the set, while “Y” means that it belongs to the set.

Some comments are in order. First, our reduction to a smaller state space has caused the policies of the agents to be (more) stochastic. Moreover, this reduction has increased the number of times that the corner case described in Appendix
D.5
takes place. Since this corner case is outside the good event, we have removed such data from
\mathcal{D}^{b}
; in this way, we improve the performances of
PIRLO
.

Observe that the behavioral cloning reward
r_{\text{BC}}
belongs to the subset and superset for all the experts. This is reasonable since it assigns positive reward only to expert’s actions in the support of the expert’s policy. However, it should be remarked that if we had not removed the “corner-case” samples, then
r_{\text{BC}}
would not belong to the subsets.

The reward
r
compatible with the analysis provided in
Likmeta et al. 2021
belongs to the superset of some experts only. Specifically, for the experts Alice, Eve, Grace, and Craig, that belong to the clusters 1 and 3 of Table 1 of
Likmeta et al. 2021
, the reward
r
is not in the superset. However, it should be remarked that reward
r
is not exactly the same as the reward described by
Likmeta et al. 2021
, and also that we are working with a more aggregated state space.

Notice that, as expected, reward
\overline{r}=-r
, which rewards “bad” actions, does not belong neither to the subset nor to the superset of any expert.
